Like if I’m in the courthouse or around the office, I’ll notice someone around my age, or even younger, and I’ll start thinking, “Her skin looks tighter,” or “Why doesn’t her face look uneven like mine?” I don’t say anything, obviously, but it’s in my head.\n\nAnd pictures… I’ve started avoiding casual pictures if I’m already feeling bad about how I look that day. If someone wants to take a quick photo, I’ll try to stand behind someone or offer to take the picture instead. I know that probably sounds silly, but in the moment it feels safer than seeing a bad angle of myself later.\n\nDoctor: How much does the way your face looks interfere with your daily life — like your job, social interactions, or other activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It does interfere, but I wouldn’t say it’s completely taking over my life. I mean, I’m still going to work, I still get my work done, and nobody at the courthouse has said anything like I’m not keeping up. But it does affect the edges of my day, if that makes sense.\n\nIn the mornings, the getting-ready part can stretch longer than I mean for it to. I’ll think I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m adjusting makeup or looking again, and suddenly I’m rushing out the door. There have been a few mornings where I’ve gotten to the courthouse feeling flustered because I cut it too close, and I hate starting the day that way.\n\nIt’s also gotten in the way of things I actually like doing. I go to yoga, and more than once I’ve missed the beginning of class because I got caught up trying to make my face look… I don’t know, acceptable, I guess, before leaving. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s yoga, people aren’t even looking at me that closely, but I still get stuck.\n\nSocially it’s not like I’ve stopped seeing people, but I have avoided some little things. Like at book club or community events, if someone wants to take a casual picture, I’ve skipped being in it or found a way to step aside because I didn’t want to spend the rest of the night worrying about how my face looked in the photo. So it’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely making normal things feel harder than they should.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror and notice your face looking uneven or saggy, do you ever think it might not be as noticeable to others as it seems to you, or do you feel certain that others would see it the same way you do?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m not completely certain other people see it the way I do, because my husband keeps saying he really doesn’t see what I’m talking about. He’ll look at me and say my face looks normal, or that he doesn’t notice anything with my cheeks or jaw.\n\nBut when I’m in that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very obvious to me. Like the skin around my cheeks and along my jaw just looks uneven, or a little saggy, and once I notice it I have a hard time believing it isn’t noticeable. So part of me knows maybe the mirror is making it seem worse, or maybe I’m focusing on it more than other people would… but in the moment, when I’m looking at it, it feels hard to trust that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel intense distress or anxiety about how your face looks, to the point where it’s hard to focus on anything else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s really why I ended up coming in. It’s not just that I notice it and move on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, and to me it looks uneven or kind of saggy. And then once I see it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it.\n\nMy husband tells me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and I know he’s trying to be reassuring, but it doesn’t always land that way. I’ll think, “How can you not see it?” because under that light it feels so obvious to me. And then I can be standing there longer than I meant to, just looking and trying to figure out if it’s really as bad as it seems.\n\nSo yes, it can make me anxious and distracted. Maybe not every single minute of the day, but when I get stuck on it, it’s hard to just shake it off and focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or fix your appearance in a certain way, like putting on makeup, even if you know it’s taking too much time or causing you stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not in some perfectly organized ritual way, but most mornings I end up checking my face more than I meant to. I’ll look straight on, then turn a little to one side, then the other side, especially around my cheeks and jaw, because I’m trying to see if it looks uneven or if one side looks worse. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just one more look,” but then it’s not just one more look.\n\nAnd then I usually put on a little extra makeup before I leave for the courthouse. I don’t wear heavy makeup exactly, but I’ll add more than I planned, trying to make things look smoother or less noticeable. Sometimes I know I’m running late or I can feel myself getting tense, but it’s hard to walk away until I feel like it’s at least acceptable enough to be around people at work.\n\nI also catch myself comparing my face to other women I see at the courthouse, which I feel embarrassed even saying. Like if I’m passing someone in the hallway or sitting near someone, I’ll notice if their skin looks firmer or their face looks more even, and then I start thinking about my own face again.\n\nAnd with pictures, if I’m already not feeling okay about how I look that day, I’ll avoid being in them. Like if it’s just a casual photo at a work thing or with a group, I might step back or offer to take the picture instead. It’s not like I want to make a scene about it, but I just don’t want to deal with seeing my face afterward and feeling upset about it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how your face looks, do you believe the changes you see — like the unevenness or sagging — are very noticeable, or do you think they might be slight or barely perceptible to other people?\n\nPatient: I think… when I’m looking at it, especially in that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very noticeable to me. Like the skin around my cheeks and my jaw just catches the light in this way where it looks uneven, or a little saggy, and then I can’t really unsee it.\n\nBut I also know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about. He’ll tell me it looks normal, or that he doesn’t notice anything. So I guess part of me understands it might be slight, or maybe not something other people would pick up on right away. But in my own mind, in the moment, it doesn’t feel slight. It feels obvious enough that I keep focusing on it, which is honestly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered if the way your face looks might be due to something medical, like a skin condition or something that a dermatologist or plastic surgeon could fix?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve thought in a general way like, “Is this just my skin changing?” or “Is this something about lighting or aging?” but I haven’t really settled on, like, a specific medical explanation.\n\nI haven’t gone to a dermatologist or a plastic surgeon about it, if that’s what you mean. I think part of me has wondered whether someone could tell me if it’s “normal” or if there’s something that could be done, but I also get embarrassed because I’m not sure if they’d even see what I’m talking about. My husband says he doesn’t, so then I think maybe I’d go in and sound ridiculous.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve wondered a little, but not in a clear “this is a skin condition” way. More like I keep trying to figure out whether it’s real enough that someone else would recognize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the unevenness or sagging you see in your cheeks and jawline is a major flaw, or do you think it’s something that most people wouldn’t notice unless you pointed it out to them?\n\nPatient: I guess that’s the part I go back and forth on. When I’m in front of that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels like a major flaw to me. Like I’ll look at my cheeks and my jawline, and the skin just looks uneven or kind of saggy, and once I see it, it feels really hard not to focus on it.\n\nBut then my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, or that it just looks like my normal face. So I know, logically, maybe most people wouldn’t notice unless I pointed it out. I’m here because I can’t seem to let it go, even if other people don’t seem to see it the way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors, like mirror checking, adjusting your appearance, or mentally going over how your face looks, in a way that feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I’d like to admit. Most mornings, especially if I’m getting ready to go to the courthouse, I’ll check my face in the mirror from a few different angles. It starts out like I’m just doing my normal routine, but then I’ll turn my head this way and that way, or lean closer, and I’ll look at my cheeks and jawline to see if that unevenness is showing.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually put on a little extra makeup, even if I already look “done,” because I feel like maybe I can soften it or cover whatever I’m seeing. Sometimes it makes me late or just puts me in a bad mood before I’ve even left the house.\n\nAt work, I catch myself comparing my face to other women’s faces, like coworkers or women I see around the courthouse. I’ll notice their skin or jawline and then think about mine, and it’s hard to stop once I’ve started. And with pictures… if someone wants to take a casual photo, I’ll avoid it sometimes if I’m not feeling okay about how I look that day. I don’t always make a big thing of it, but I’ll kind of step out or offer to take the picture instead. It feels a little embarrassing saying it out loud, but yes, it does feel hard to control sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about the specific areas of your face — your cheeks and jawline — or do you also feel anxious about other parts of your body or appearance as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those areas of my face, yes — my cheeks and my jawline. I don’t really sit there worrying about, like, my whole body or every feature. It’s more that since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing this unevenness around my cheeks, and then the jaw area looks kind of saggy to me, or not as smooth as it should be.\n\nAnd I know I keep coming back to the mirror thing, but that really is when it started standing out to me more. Before that, I’m sure I had normal little concerns here and there, like anyone does, but this feels more specific. I’ll look in that light and think, “How did I not notice this before?” And then once I see it, it’s like I carry it with me.\n\nMy husband says he honestly can’t see what I’m talking about, which makes me feel a little foolish, but it doesn’t completely reassure me. So, no, it’s not really other parts of my body. It’s mostly the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or saggy to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the unevenness or sagging you see around your cheeks and jaw is due to something like a medical condition, aging, or a structural change in your face, or do you feel it might be more about how you’re perceiving it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate those things, honestly. Part of me wants to say, “Well, I’m 46, maybe this is just my face changing,” or maybe the lighting is showing things that were always there. But I haven’t had some medical explanation for it or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do know is that ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. It looks uneven to me, or kind of saggy, and then once I notice it, I can’t unsee it. My husband keeps saying he really can’t see what I’m talking about, or that it doesn’t look different to him, so that makes me think maybe it’s more about how I’m seeing it than something obvious that’s actually there.\n\nBut it still feels real when I’m looking at it. That’s the frustrating part. I can understand, in my head, that maybe other people aren’t seeing this big change, but when I’m standing there in that mirror, it looks very noticeable to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to spend a lot of time trying to fix or hide the way your cheeks and jawline look, even if you know it might not make a big difference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know on some level that putting on a little more makeup probably isn’t going to magically change my jawline or whatever I’m seeing in my cheeks, but I still end up doing it.\n\nMost mornings, especially if I’m going to the courthouse, I’ll check my face from a few different angles before I leave. I’ll look straight on, then turn a little to one side, then the other, and sometimes I get closer to the mirror like I’m trying to figure out if it’s really as uneven as it looks. And then I’ll add a bit more makeup, usually around my cheeks or along the jaw area, even if I already had enough on. It’s like I’m trying to soften it somehow, or make it less noticeable, even though I’m not sure it actually helps.\n\nAnd then once I’m at work, it doesn’t always stop. I’ll notice other women’s faces — coworkers, people coming in and out of the courthouse — and I’ll compare my cheeks or jawline to theirs. I don’t mean to do it, but it just happens, and then I feel worse about mine.\n\nWith pictures, too, if I’m already feeling bad about how my face looks that day, I’ll try to avoid being in casual photos. I’ll offer to take the picture instead or just kind of step out of it. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably seems small, but in the moment it feels like I need to do something to hide it or avoid having it captured.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way your cheeks and jawline look is something that others would immediately notice and judge, or do you think it’s something only you are particularly sensitive to noticing?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s probably something I’m especially sensitive to noticing, if I’m being honest. Because my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, and he’s not usually someone who just says things to make me feel better. He’ll be polite, obviously, but he seems genuinely confused when I point out my cheeks or the jaw area.\n\nBut when I’m looking in that brighter bathroom mirror, it doesn’t feel subtle to me. It’s like the skin around my cheeks looks uneven, and then along my jaw it looks kind of saggy or not as firm as it should, and I start thinking, “Surely other people can see this.” Especially if I’m under bright lights or talking close to someone.\n\nSo I guess my logical answer is that maybe other people aren’t immediately noticing it the way I am. But emotionally, in the moment, it feels very noticeable, like it’s right there on my face and people could judge it, even if they don’t say anything. That’s part of what makes it hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: When you're upset about how your face looks, do you ever experience sudden, intense episodes of anxiety or panic, like your heart racing, feeling short of breath, or fearing something terrible will happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. I definitely get upset, and I can feel really tense or embarrassed, like I want to fix it right away or get away from the mirror, but I don’t think I’ve had those sudden episodes where my heart is racing and I can’t breathe or I think something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I get stuck on it mentally. I’ll look at my face and feel this wave of dread or shame, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like a full panic attack. I’m still able to go to work or leave the house, even if I’m uncomfortable and thinking about it too much. So… no, I haven’t really noticed shortness of breath or that kind of intense panic. It’s more distressing and consuming than physically panicky, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It hasn’t stopped me from functioning completely, no. I mean, I still go to work, and I still get my assignments done. I don’t think anyone at the courthouse would say I’m not doing my job or anything like that.

But it does affect things around the edges, if that makes sense. Like in the mornings, if I get caught in the mirror too long trying to decide whether my cheeks look uneven or whether I need to add more makeup along my jaw, I can lose track of time. Then I’m rushing into the courthouse, feeling flustered before the day even starts. That’s happened more than a few times.

And with yoga, I’ve missed the beginning of class more than once because I was still at home fussing with my face or checking it again before I left. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because it’s not like those extra few minutes actually change much, but in the moment it feels hard to just stop and go.

Socially, I don’t usually avoid whole events, but I have avoided being in pictures. At book club or community events, if someone pulls out a phone for a casual photo, I’ll sometimes step aside or offer to take it instead, because I don’t want to spend the rest of the night worrying about how my face looked in it. So it’s not ruining my life, exactly, but it does interfere more than I’d like to admit.

At book club or community events, if someone pulls out a phone for a casual photo, I’ll sometimes step aside or offer to take it instead, because I don’t want to spend the rest of the night worrying about how my face looked in it. I don’t really sit there worrying about, like, my whole body or every feature. It’s more that since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing this unevenness around my cheeks, and then the jaw area looks kind of saggy to me, or not as smooth as it should be.\n\nAnd I know I keep coming back to the mirror thing, but that really is when it started standing out to me more. Before that, I’m sure I had normal little concerns here and there, like anyone does, but this feels more specific. I’ll look in that light and think, “How did I not notice this before?” And then once I see it, it’s like I carry it with me.\n\nMy husband says he honestly can’t see what I’m talking about, which makes me feel a little foolish, but it doesn’t completely reassure me. So, no, it’s not really other parts of my body. It’s mostly the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or saggy to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the unevenness or sagging you see around your cheeks and jaw is due to something like a medical condition, aging, or a structural change in your face, or do you feel it might be more about how you’re perceiving it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate those things, honestly. Part of me wants to say, “Well, I’m 46, maybe this is just my face changing,” or maybe the lighting is showing things that were always there. But I haven’t had some medical explanation for it or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do know is that ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. That’s the frustrating part. I mean, I know on some level that putting on a little more makeup probably isn’t going to magically change my jawline or whatever I’m seeing in my cheeks, but I still end up doing it.\n\nMost mornings, especially if I’m going to the courthouse, I’ll check my face from a few different angles before I leave. And then I’ll add a bit more makeup, usually around my cheeks or along the jaw area, even if I already had enough on. I definitely get upset, and I can feel really tense or embarrassed, like I want to fix it right away or get away from the mirror, but I don’t think I’ve had those sudden episodes where my heart is racing and I can’t breathe or I think something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I get stuck on it mentally. I’ll look at my face and feel this wave of dread or shame, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like a full panic attack. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I know “abnormal” sounds like a strong word, and part of me feels embarrassed saying that, because my husband keeps telling me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. But when I’m looking at myself, especially since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, it really does look like a flaw to me.

It’s mostly around my cheeks and along my jawline. The skin looks uneven to me, or kind of saggy in a way that makes my face look less attractive than it should. I’ll stand there and think, “How can he not see this?” even though he seems genuinely unable to see it the way I do. So I guess, yes, I do believe it’s a defect or something wrong with how my face looks, at least when I’m focused on it.

I don’t know if other people would call it abnormal. Maybe they wouldn’t. But to me, in that mirror, it feels obvious and really unattractive.

Like straight on, then turning my head a little, sometimes looking under the brighter light and then stepping back to see if it still looks the same. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting ready, but it kind of turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then I usually put on a little more makeup than I meant to, mostly around my cheeks and jaw area, just trying to make it look smoother or less noticeable. It’s not like I’m spending hours and hours, but it’s enough that I notice I’m doing it, and sometimes it makes me run later than I planned.\n\nI also catch myself comparing my face to other women at work, which makes me feel awful saying that. Like if I’m in the courthouse or around the office, I’ll notice someone around my age, or even younger, and I’ll start thinking, “Her skin looks tighter,” or “Why doesn’t her face look uneven like mine?” I don’t say anything, obviously, but it’s in my head.\n\nAnd pictures… I’ve started avoiding casual pictures if I’m already feeling bad about how I look that day. If someone wants to take a quick photo, I’ll try to stand behind someone or offer to take the picture instead. I know that probably sounds silly, but in the moment it feels safer than seeing a bad angle of myself later.\n\nDoctor: How much does the way your face looks interfere with your daily life — like your job, social interactions, or other activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It does interfere, but I wouldn’t say it’s completely taking over my life. I mean, I’m still going to work, I still get my work done, and nobody at the courthouse has said anything like I’m not keeping up. But it does affect the edges of my day, if that makes sense.\n\nIn the mornings, the getting-ready part can stretch longer than I mean for it to. I’ll think I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m adjusting makeup or looking again, and suddenly I’m rushing out the door. There have been a few mornings where I’ve gotten to the courthouse feeling flustered because I cut it too close, and I hate starting the day that way.\n\nIt’s also gotten in the way of things I actually like doing. I go to yoga, and more than once I’ve missed the beginning of class because I got caught up trying to make my face look… I don’t know, acceptable, I guess, before leaving. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s yoga, people aren’t even looking at me that closely, but I still get stuck.\n\nSocially it’s not like I’ve stopped seeing people, but I have avoided some little things. Like at book club or community events, if someone wants to take a casual picture, I’ve skipped being in it or found a way to step aside because I didn’t want to spend the rest of the night worrying about how my face looked in the photo. So it’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely making normal things feel harder than they should.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror and notice your face looking uneven or saggy, do you ever think it might not be as noticeable to others as it seems to you, or do you feel certain that others would see it the same way you do?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m not completely certain other people see it the way I do, because my husband keeps saying he really doesn’t see what I’m talking about. He’ll look at me and say my face looks normal, or that he doesn’t notice anything with my cheeks or jaw.\n\nBut when I’m in that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very obvious to me. Like the skin around my cheeks and along my jaw just looks uneven, or a little saggy, and once I notice it I have a hard time believing it isn’t noticeable. So part of me knows maybe the mirror is making it seem worse, or maybe I’m focusing on it more than other people would… but in the moment, when I’m looking at it, it feels hard to trust that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel intense distress or anxiety about how your face looks, to the point where it’s hard to focus on anything else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s really why I ended up coming in. It’s not just that I notice it and move on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, and to me it looks uneven or kind of saggy. And then once I see it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it.\n\nMy husband tells me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and I know he’s trying to be reassuring, but it doesn’t always land that way. I’ll think, “How can you not see it?” because under that light it feels so obvious to me. And then I can be standing there longer than I meant to, just looking and trying to figure out if it’s really as bad as it seems.\n\nSo yes, it can make me anxious and distracted. Maybe not every single minute of the day, but when I get stuck on it, it’s hard to just shake it off and focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or fix your appearance in a certain way, like putting on makeup, even if you know it’s taking too much time or causing you stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not in some perfectly organized ritual way, but most mornings I end up checking my face more than I meant to. I’ll look straight on, then turn a little to one side, then the other side, especially around my cheeks and jaw, because I’m trying to see if it looks uneven or if one side looks worse. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just one more look,” but then it’s not just one more look.\n\nAnd then I usually put on a little extra makeup before I leave for the courthouse. I don’t wear heavy makeup exactly, but I’ll add more than I planned, trying to make things look smoother or less noticeable. Sometimes I know I’m running late or I can feel myself getting tense, but it’s hard to walk away until I feel like it’s at least acceptable enough to be around people at work.\n\nI also catch myself comparing my face to other women I see at the courthouse, which I feel embarrassed even saying. Like if I’m passing someone in the hallway or sitting near someone, I’ll notice if their skin looks firmer or their face looks more even, and then I start thinking about my own face again.\n\nAnd with pictures, if I’m already not feeling okay about how I look that day, I’ll avoid being in them. Like if it’s just a casual photo at a work thing or with a group, I might step back or offer to take the picture instead. It’s not like I want to make a scene about it, but I just don’t want to deal with seeing my face afterward and feeling upset about it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how your face looks, do you believe the changes you see — like the unevenness or sagging — are very noticeable, or do you think they might be slight or barely perceptible to other people?\n\nPatient: I think… when I’m looking at it, especially in that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very noticeable to me. Like the skin around my cheeks and my jaw just catches the light in this way where it looks uneven, or a little saggy, and then I can’t really unsee it.\n\nBut I also know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about. He’ll tell me it looks normal, or that he doesn’t notice anything. So I guess part of me understands it might be slight, or maybe not something other people would pick up on right away. But in my own mind, in the moment, it doesn’t feel slight. It feels obvious enough that I keep focusing on it, which is honestly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered if the way your face looks might be due to something medical, like a skin condition or something that a dermatologist or plastic surgeon could fix?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve thought in a general way like, “Is this just my skin changing?” or “Is this something about lighting or aging?” but I haven’t really settled on, like, a specific medical explanation.\n\nI haven’t gone to a dermatologist or a plastic surgeon about it, if that’s what you mean. I think part of me has wondered whether someone could tell me if it’s “normal” or if there’s something that could be done, but I also get embarrassed because I’m not sure if they’d even see what I’m talking about. My husband says he doesn’t, so then I think maybe I’d go in and sound ridiculous.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve wondered a little, but not in a clear “this is a skin condition” way. More like I keep trying to figure out whether it’s real enough that someone else would recognize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the unevenness or sagging you see in your cheeks and jawline is a major flaw, or do you think it’s something that most people wouldn’t notice unless you pointed it out to them?\n\nPatient: I guess that’s the part I go back and forth on. When I’m in front of that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels like a major flaw to me. Like I’ll look at my cheeks and my jawline, and the skin just looks uneven or kind of saggy, and once I see it, it feels really hard not to focus on it.\n\nBut then my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, or that it just looks like my normal face. So I know, logically, maybe most people wouldn’t notice unless I pointed it out. I’m here because I can’t seem to let it go, even if other people don’t seem to see it the way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors, like mirror checking, adjusting your appearance, or mentally going over how your face looks, in a way that feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I’d like to admit. Most mornings, especially if I’m getting ready to go to the courthouse, I’ll check my face in the mirror from a few different angles. It starts out like I’m just doing my normal routine, but then I’ll turn my head this way and that way, or lean closer, and I’ll look at my cheeks and jawline to see if that unevenness is showing.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually put on a little extra makeup, even if I already look “done,” because I feel like maybe I can soften it or cover whatever I’m seeing. Sometimes it makes me late or just puts me in a bad mood before I’ve even left the house.\n\nAt work, I catch myself comparing my face to other women’s faces, like coworkers or women I see around the courthouse. I’ll notice their skin or jawline and then think about mine, and it’s hard to stop once I’ve started. And with pictures… if someone wants to take a casual photo, I’ll avoid it sometimes if I’m not feeling okay about how I look that day. I don’t always make a big thing of it, but I’ll kind of step out or offer to take the picture instead. It feels a little embarrassing saying it out loud, but yes, it does feel hard to control sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about the specific areas of your face — your cheeks and jawline — or do you also feel anxious about other parts of your body or appearance as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those areas of my face, yes — my cheeks and my jawline. I don’t really sit there worrying about, like, my whole body or every feature. It’s more that since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing this unevenness around my cheeks, and then the jaw area looks kind of saggy to me, or not as smooth as it should be.\n\nAnd I know I keep coming back to the mirror thing, but that really is when it started standing out to me more. Before that, I’m sure I had normal little concerns here and there, like anyone does, but this feels more specific. I’ll look in that light and think, “How did I not notice this before?” And then once I see it, it’s like I carry it with me.\n\nMy husband says he honestly can’t see what I’m talking about, which makes me feel a little foolish, but it doesn’t completely reassure me. So, no, it’s not really other parts of my body. It’s mostly the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or saggy to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the unevenness or sagging you see around your cheeks and jaw is due to something like a medical condition, aging, or a structural change in your face, or do you feel it might be more about how you’re perceiving it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate those things, honestly. Part of me wants to say, “Well, I’m 46, maybe this is just my face changing,” or maybe the lighting is showing things that were always there. But I haven’t had some medical explanation for it or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do know is that ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. It looks uneven to me, or kind of saggy, and then once I notice it, I can’t unsee it. My husband keeps saying he really can’t see what I’m talking about, or that it doesn’t look different to him, so that makes me think maybe it’s more about how I’m seeing it than something obvious that’s actually there.\n\nBut it still feels real when I’m looking at it. That’s the frustrating part. I can understand, in my head, that maybe other people aren’t seeing this big change, but when I’m standing there in that mirror, it looks very noticeable to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to spend a lot of time trying to fix or hide the way your cheeks and jawline look, even if you know it might not make a big difference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know on some level that putting on a little more makeup probably isn’t going to magically change my jawline or whatever I’m seeing in my cheeks, but I still end up doing it.\n\nMost mornings, especially if I’m going to the courthouse, I’ll check my face from a few different angles before I leave. I’ll look straight on, then turn a little to one side, then the other, and sometimes I get closer to the mirror like I’m trying to figure out if it’s really as uneven as it looks. And then I’ll add a bit more makeup, usually around my cheeks or along the jaw area, even if I already had enough on. It’s like I’m trying to soften it somehow, or make it less noticeable, even though I’m not sure it actually helps.\n\nAnd then once I’m at work, it doesn’t always stop. I’ll notice other women’s faces — coworkers, people coming in and out of the courthouse — and I’ll compare my cheeks or jawline to theirs. I don’t mean to do it, but it just happens, and then I feel worse about mine.\n\nWith pictures, too, if I’m already feeling bad about how my face looks that day, I’ll try to avoid being in casual photos. I’ll offer to take the picture instead or just kind of step out of it. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably seems small, but in the moment it feels like I need to do something to hide it or avoid having it captured.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way your cheeks and jawline look is something that others would immediately notice and judge, or do you think it’s something only you are particularly sensitive to noticing?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s probably something I’m especially sensitive to noticing, if I’m being honest. Because my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, and he’s not usually someone who just says things to make me feel better. He’ll be polite, obviously, but he seems genuinely confused when I point out my cheeks or the jaw area.\n\nBut when I’m looking in that brighter bathroom mirror, it doesn’t feel subtle to me. It’s like the skin around my cheeks looks uneven, and then along my jaw it looks kind of saggy or not as firm as it should, and I start thinking, “Surely other people can see this.” Especially if I’m under bright lights or talking close to someone.\n\nSo I guess my logical answer is that maybe other people aren’t immediately noticing it the way I am. But emotionally, in the moment, it feels very noticeable, like it’s right there on my face and people could judge it, even if they don’t say anything. That’s part of what makes it hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: When you're upset about how your face looks, do you ever experience sudden, intense episodes of anxiety or panic, like your heart racing, feeling short of breath, or fearing something terrible will happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. I definitely get upset, and I can feel really tense or embarrassed, like I want to fix it right away or get away from the mirror, but I don’t think I’ve had those sudden episodes where my heart is racing and I can’t breathe or I think something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I get stuck on it mentally. I’ll look at my face and feel this wave of dread or shame, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like a full panic attack. I’m still able to go to work or leave the house, even if I’m uncomfortable and thinking about it too much. So… no, I haven’t really noticed shortness of breath or that kind of intense panic. It’s more distressing and consuming than physically panicky, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your cheeks and jawline ever caused you to feel so distressed that it affected your ability to function normally, such as difficulty concentrating at work, feeling too embarrassed to go out, or avoiding social situations altogether?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from functioning completely, no. I mean, I still go to work, and I still get my assignments done. I don’t think anyone at the courthouse would say I’m not doing my job or anything like that.\n\nBut it does affect things around the edges, if that makes sense. Like in the mornings, if I get caught in the mirror too long trying to decide whether my cheeks look uneven or whether I need to add more makeup along my jaw, I can lose track of time. Then I’m rushing into the courthouse, feeling flustered before the day even starts. That’s happened more than a few times.\n\nAnd with yoga, I’ve missed the beginning of class more than once because I was still at home fussing with my face or checking it again before I left. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because it’s not like those extra few minutes actually change much, but in the moment it feels hard to just stop and go.\n\nSocially, I don’t usually avoid whole events, but I have avoided being in pictures. At book club or community events, if someone pulls out a phone for a casual photo, I’ll sometimes step aside or offer to take it instead, because I don’t want to spend the rest of the night worrying about how my face looked in it. So it’s not ruining my life, exactly, but it does interfere more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the way your cheeks and jawline look is a defect or flaw that makes your face appear abnormal or unattractive, even if others don’t seem to notice it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know “abnormal” sounds like a strong word, and part of me feels embarrassed saying that, because my husband keeps telling me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. But when I’m looking at myself, especially since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, it really does look like a flaw to me.\n\nIt’s mostly around my cheeks and along my jawline. The skin looks uneven to me, or kind of saggy in a way that makes my face look less attractive than it should. I’ll stand there and think, “How can he not see this?” even though he seems genuinely unable to see it the way I do. So I guess, yes, I do believe it’s a defect or something wrong with how my face looks, at least when I’m focused on it.\n\nI don’t know if other people would call it abnormal. Maybe they wouldn’t. But to me, in that mirror, it feels obvious and really unattractive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll stand there and think, “How can he not see this?” even though he seems genuinely unable to see it the way I do.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say it makes me feel unlovable, exactly. That word feels… bigger than what I mean. I know my husband loves me, and he keeps saying he can’t really see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel more confused or embarrassed because to me it’s so noticeable.

It’s more that my face feels unacceptable to me in those moments. Especially since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing the skin around my cheeks and jawline as uneven or kind of saggy, and then I can’t unsee it. I’ll think, “That doesn’t look right,” or “My face looks wrong today,” even if someone else would probably say it looks normal.

So… not unlovable, no. But unacceptable to me sometimes, yes. Like I feel dissatisfied or almost bothered by my own reflection, even when other people don’t seem to notice anything.

So that’s mostly why I’m here, because I’m not sure if I’m overreacting or if there’s something wrong, but it’s been on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking the mirror, touching your face, or comparing yourself to others, because of how you feel about your facial appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably want to admit. Most mornings, especially before I leave for the courthouse, I end up checking my face in the mirror from a few different angles. Like straight on, then turning my head a little, sometimes looking under the brighter light and then stepping back to see if it still looks the same. I’ll tell myself I’m just getting ready, but it kind of turns into this whole thing.\n\nAnd then I usually put on a little more makeup than I meant to, mostly around my cheeks and jaw area, just trying to make it look smoother or less noticeable. It’s not like I’m spending hours and hours, but it’s enough that I notice I’m doing it, and sometimes it makes me run later than I planned.\n\nI also catch myself comparing my face to other women at work, which makes me feel awful saying that. Like if I’m in the courthouse or around the office, I’ll notice someone around my age, or even younger, and I’ll start thinking, “Her skin looks tighter,” or “Why doesn’t her face look uneven like mine?” I don’t say anything, obviously, but it’s in my head.\n\nAnd pictures… I’ve started avoiding casual pictures if I’m already feeling bad about how I look that day. If someone wants to take a quick photo, I’ll try to stand behind someone or offer to take the picture instead. I know that probably sounds silly, but in the moment it feels safer than seeing a bad angle of myself later.\n\nDoctor: How much does the way your face looks interfere with your daily life — like your job, social interactions, or other activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It does interfere, but I wouldn’t say it’s completely taking over my life. I mean, I’m still going to work, I still get my work done, and nobody at the courthouse has said anything like I’m not keeping up. But it does affect the edges of my day, if that makes sense.\n\nIn the mornings, the getting-ready part can stretch longer than I mean for it to. I’ll think I’m just going to check quickly, and then I’m adjusting makeup or looking again, and suddenly I’m rushing out the door. There have been a few mornings where I’ve gotten to the courthouse feeling flustered because I cut it too close, and I hate starting the day that way.\n\nIt’s also gotten in the way of things I actually like doing. I go to yoga, and more than once I’ve missed the beginning of class because I got caught up trying to make my face look… I don’t know, acceptable, I guess, before leaving. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s yoga, people aren’t even looking at me that closely, but I still get stuck.\n\nSocially it’s not like I’ve stopped seeing people, but I have avoided some little things. Like at book club or community events, if someone wants to take a casual picture, I’ve skipped being in it or found a way to step aside because I didn’t want to spend the rest of the night worrying about how my face looked in the photo. So it’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely making normal things feel harder than they should.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror and notice your face looking uneven or saggy, do you ever think it might not be as noticeable to others as it seems to you, or do you feel certain that others would see it the same way you do?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m not completely certain other people see it the way I do, because my husband keeps saying he really doesn’t see what I’m talking about. He’ll look at me and say my face looks normal, or that he doesn’t notice anything with my cheeks or jaw.\n\nBut when I’m in that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very obvious to me. Like the skin around my cheeks and along my jaw just looks uneven, or a little saggy, and once I notice it I have a hard time believing it isn’t noticeable. So part of me knows maybe the mirror is making it seem worse, or maybe I’m focusing on it more than other people would… but in the moment, when I’m looking at it, it feels hard to trust that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel intense distress or anxiety about how your face looks, to the point where it’s hard to focus on anything else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s really why I ended up coming in. It’s not just that I notice it and move on. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing the skin around my cheeks and jaw, and to me it looks uneven or kind of saggy. And then once I see it, it’s like my brain keeps going back to it.\n\nMy husband tells me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and I know he’s trying to be reassuring, but it doesn’t always land that way. I’ll think, “How can you not see it?” because under that light it feels so obvious to me. And then I can be standing there longer than I meant to, just looking and trying to figure out if it’s really as bad as it seems.\n\nSo yes, it can make me anxious and distracted. Maybe not every single minute of the day, but when I get stuck on it, it’s hard to just shake it off and focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or fix your appearance in a certain way, like putting on makeup, even if you know it’s taking too much time or causing you stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not in some perfectly organized ritual way, but most mornings I end up checking my face more than I meant to. I’ll look straight on, then turn a little to one side, then the other side, especially around my cheeks and jaw, because I’m trying to see if it looks uneven or if one side looks worse. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just one more look,” but then it’s not just one more look.\n\nAnd then I usually put on a little extra makeup before I leave for the courthouse. I don’t wear heavy makeup exactly, but I’ll add more than I planned, trying to make things look smoother or less noticeable. Sometimes I know I’m running late or I can feel myself getting tense, but it’s hard to walk away until I feel like it’s at least acceptable enough to be around people at work.\n\nI also catch myself comparing my face to other women I see at the courthouse, which I feel embarrassed even saying. Like if I’m passing someone in the hallway or sitting near someone, I’ll notice if their skin looks firmer or their face looks more even, and then I start thinking about my own face again.\n\nAnd with pictures, if I’m already not feeling okay about how I look that day, I’ll avoid being in them. Like if it’s just a casual photo at a work thing or with a group, I might step back or offer to take the picture instead. It’s not like I want to make a scene about it, but I just don’t want to deal with seeing my face afterward and feeling upset about it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how your face looks, do you believe the changes you see — like the unevenness or sagging — are very noticeable, or do you think they might be slight or barely perceptible to other people?\n\nPatient: I think… when I’m looking at it, especially in that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very noticeable to me. Like the skin around my cheeks and my jaw just catches the light in this way where it looks uneven, or a little saggy, and then I can’t really unsee it.\n\nBut I also know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about. He’ll tell me it looks normal, or that he doesn’t notice anything. So I guess part of me understands it might be slight, or maybe not something other people would pick up on right away. But in my own mind, in the moment, it doesn’t feel slight. It feels obvious enough that I keep focusing on it, which is honestly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered if the way your face looks might be due to something medical, like a skin condition or something that a dermatologist or plastic surgeon could fix?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve thought in a general way like, “Is this just my skin changing?” or “Is this something about lighting or aging?” but I haven’t really settled on, like, a specific medical explanation.\n\nI haven’t gone to a dermatologist or a plastic surgeon about it, if that’s what you mean. I think part of me has wondered whether someone could tell me if it’s “normal” or if there’s something that could be done, but I also get embarrassed because I’m not sure if they’d even see what I’m talking about. My husband says he doesn’t, so then I think maybe I’d go in and sound ridiculous.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve wondered a little, but not in a clear “this is a skin condition” way. More like I keep trying to figure out whether it’s real enough that someone else would recognize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the unevenness or sagging you see in your cheeks and jawline is a major flaw, or do you think it’s something that most people wouldn’t notice unless you pointed it out to them?\n\nPatient: I guess that’s the part I go back and forth on. When I’m in front of that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels like a major flaw to me. Like I’ll look at my cheeks and my jawline, and the skin just looks uneven or kind of saggy, and once I see it, it feels really hard not to focus on it.\n\nBut then my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, or that it just looks like my normal face. So I know, logically, maybe most people wouldn’t notice unless I pointed it out. I’m here because I can’t seem to let it go, even if other people don’t seem to see it the way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors, like mirror checking, adjusting your appearance, or mentally going over how your face looks, in a way that feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I’d like to admit. Most mornings, especially if I’m getting ready to go to the courthouse, I’ll check my face in the mirror from a few different angles. It starts out like I’m just doing my normal routine, but then I’ll turn my head this way and that way, or lean closer, and I’ll look at my cheeks and jawline to see if that unevenness is showing.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually put on a little extra makeup, even if I already look “done,” because I feel like maybe I can soften it or cover whatever I’m seeing. Sometimes it makes me late or just puts me in a bad mood before I’ve even left the house.\n\nAt work, I catch myself comparing my face to other women’s faces, like coworkers or women I see around the courthouse. I’ll notice their skin or jawline and then think about mine, and it’s hard to stop once I’ve started. And with pictures… if someone wants to take a casual photo, I’ll avoid it sometimes if I’m not feeling okay about how I look that day. I don’t always make a big thing of it, but I’ll kind of step out or offer to take the picture instead. It feels a little embarrassing saying it out loud, but yes, it does feel hard to control sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about the specific areas of your face — your cheeks and jawline — or do you also feel anxious about other parts of your body or appearance as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those areas of my face, yes — my cheeks and my jawline. I don’t really sit there worrying about, like, my whole body or every feature. It’s more that since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing this unevenness around my cheeks, and then the jaw area looks kind of saggy to me, or not as smooth as it should be.\n\nAnd I know I keep coming back to the mirror thing, but that really is when it started standing out to me more. Before that, I’m sure I had normal little concerns here and there, like anyone does, but this feels more specific. I’ll look in that light and think, “How did I not notice this before?” And then once I see it, it’s like I carry it with me.\n\nMy husband says he honestly can’t see what I’m talking about, which makes me feel a little foolish, but it doesn’t completely reassure me. So, no, it’s not really other parts of my body. It’s mostly the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or saggy to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the unevenness or sagging you see around your cheeks and jaw is due to something like a medical condition, aging, or a structural change in your face, or do you feel it might be more about how you’re perceiving it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate those things, honestly. Part of me wants to say, “Well, I’m 46, maybe this is just my face changing,” or maybe the lighting is showing things that were always there. But I haven’t had some medical explanation for it or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do know is that ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and my jaw. It looks uneven to me, or kind of saggy, and then once I notice it, I can’t unsee it. My husband keeps saying he really can’t see what I’m talking about, or that it doesn’t look different to him, so that makes me think maybe it’s more about how I’m seeing it than something obvious that’s actually there.\n\nBut it still feels real when I’m looking at it. That’s the frustrating part. I can understand, in my head, that maybe other people aren’t seeing this big change, but when I’m standing there in that mirror, it looks very noticeable to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to spend a lot of time trying to fix or hide the way your cheeks and jawline look, even if you know it might not make a big difference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know on some level that putting on a little more makeup probably isn’t going to magically change my jawline or whatever I’m seeing in my cheeks, but I still end up doing it.\n\nMost mornings, especially if I’m going to the courthouse, I’ll check my face from a few different angles before I leave. I’ll look straight on, then turn a little to one side, then the other, and sometimes I get closer to the mirror like I’m trying to figure out if it’s really as uneven as it looks. And then I’ll add a bit more makeup, usually around my cheeks or along the jaw area, even if I already had enough on. It’s like I’m trying to soften it somehow, or make it less noticeable, even though I’m not sure it actually helps.\n\nAnd then once I’m at work, it doesn’t always stop. I’ll notice other women’s faces — coworkers, people coming in and out of the courthouse — and I’ll compare my cheeks or jawline to theirs. I don’t mean to do it, but it just happens, and then I feel worse about mine.\n\nWith pictures, too, if I’m already feeling bad about how my face looks that day, I’ll try to avoid being in casual photos. I’ll offer to take the picture instead or just kind of step out of it. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably seems small, but in the moment it feels like I need to do something to hide it or avoid having it captured.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the way your cheeks and jawline look is something that others would immediately notice and judge, or do you think it’s something only you are particularly sensitive to noticing?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it’s probably something I’m especially sensitive to noticing, if I’m being honest. Because my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, and he’s not usually someone who just says things to make me feel better. He’ll be polite, obviously, but he seems genuinely confused when I point out my cheeks or the jaw area.\n\nBut when I’m looking in that brighter bathroom mirror, it doesn’t feel subtle to me. It’s like the skin around my cheeks looks uneven, and then along my jaw it looks kind of saggy or not as firm as it should, and I start thinking, “Surely other people can see this.” Especially if I’m under bright lights or talking close to someone.\n\nSo I guess my logical answer is that maybe other people aren’t immediately noticing it the way I am. But emotionally, in the moment, it feels very noticeable, like it’s right there on my face and people could judge it, even if they don’t say anything. That’s part of what makes it hard to let go of.\n\nDoctor: When you're upset about how your face looks, do you ever experience sudden, intense episodes of anxiety or panic, like your heart racing, feeling short of breath, or fearing something terrible will happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. I definitely get upset, and I can feel really tense or embarrassed, like I want to fix it right away or get away from the mirror, but I don’t think I’ve had those sudden episodes where my heart is racing and I can’t breathe or I think something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I get stuck on it mentally. I’ll look at my face and feel this wave of dread or shame, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like a full panic attack. I’m still able to go to work or leave the house, even if I’m uncomfortable and thinking about it too much. So… no, I haven’t really noticed shortness of breath or that kind of intense panic. It’s more distressing and consuming than physically panicky, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your cheeks and jawline ever caused you to feel so distressed that it affected your ability to function normally, such as difficulty concentrating at work, feeling too embarrassed to go out, or avoiding social situations altogether?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from functioning completely, no. I mean, I still go to work, and I still get my assignments done. I don’t think anyone at the courthouse would say I’m not doing my job or anything like that.\n\nBut it does affect things around the edges, if that makes sense. Like in the mornings, if I get caught in the mirror too long trying to decide whether my cheeks look uneven or whether I need to add more makeup along my jaw, I can lose track of time. Then I’m rushing into the courthouse, feeling flustered before the day even starts. That’s happened more than a few times.\n\nAnd with yoga, I’ve missed the beginning of class more than once because I was still at home fussing with my face or checking it again before I left. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because it’s not like those extra few minutes actually change much, but in the moment it feels hard to just stop and go.\n\nSocially, I don’t usually avoid whole events, but I have avoided being in pictures. At book club or community events, if someone pulls out a phone for a casual photo, I’ll sometimes step aside or offer to take it instead, because I don’t want to spend the rest of the night worrying about how my face looked in it. So it’s not ruining my life, exactly, but it does interfere more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the way your cheeks and jawline look is a defect or flaw that makes your face appear abnormal or unattractive, even if others don’t seem to notice it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know “abnormal” sounds like a strong word, and part of me feels embarrassed saying that, because my husband keeps telling me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. But when I’m looking at myself, especially since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, it really does look like a flaw to me.\n\nIt’s mostly around my cheeks and along my jawline. The skin looks uneven to me, or kind of saggy in a way that makes my face look less attractive than it should. I’ll stand there and think, “How can he not see this?” even though he seems genuinely unable to see it the way I do. So I guess, yes, I do believe it’s a defect or something wrong with how my face looks, at least when I’m focused on it.\n\nI don’t know if other people would call it abnormal. Maybe they wouldn’t. But to me, in that mirror, it feels obvious and really unattractive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the way your cheeks and jawline look makes your overall appearance unacceptable or unlovable, even if others don’t seem to notice the same thing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it makes me feel unlovable, exactly. That word feels… bigger than what I mean. I know my husband loves me, and he keeps saying he can’t really see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel more confused or embarrassed because to me it’s so noticeable.\n\nIt’s more that my face feels unacceptable to me in those moments. Especially since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing the skin around my cheeks and jawline as uneven or kind of saggy, and then I can’t unsee it. I’ll think, “That doesn’t look right,” or “My face looks wrong today,” even if someone else would probably say it looks normal.\n\nSo… not unlovable, no. But unacceptable to me sometimes, yes. Like I feel dissatisfied or almost bothered by my own reflection, even when other people don’t seem to notice anything."
